Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Introduction••
	• The text is Jonathan Edwards' "Freedom of the Will" (1754)
	• Modernized and corrected by William H. Gross (2013)
	• Includes additional material, such as reflections and excerpts from other works
• ••Part I: Various Terms Defined••
	• Section I: Concerning the Nature of the Will (pp. 1-3)
		- Edwards defines the will and its relationship to the understanding
	• Section II: Concerning the Determination of the Will (pp. 3-8)
		- Edwards discusses the will's ability to determine its own actions
	• Section III: Concerning Necessity, Impossibility, and Inability (pp. 8-12)
		- Edwards defines and distinguishes between these concepts
	• Section IV: Distinctions between Natural and Moral Necessity (pp. 12-16)
		- Edwards explores the differences between these two types of necessity
	• Section V: Concerning Liberty and Moral Agency (pp. 16-19)
		- Edwards discusses the nature of liberty and moral agency
• ••Part II: Is the Arminian Notion of Freedom of Will the Liberty of All Moral Agents?••
	• Section I: The Inconsistency of the Arminian Notion of Liberty of Will (pp. 19-21)
		- Edwards argues that the Arminian view of free will is inconsistent
	• Section II: Ways to Evade the Foregoing Reasoning (pp. 21-24)
		- Edwards responds to potential counterarguments
	• Section III: Can Volition Arise Without a Cause? (pp. 24-27)
		- Edwards discusses the possibility of volition arising without a cause
	• Section IV: The Irrelevance of Arminian Arguments (pp. 27-30)
		- Edwards argues that Arminian arguments are inconsistent
	• Section V: The Will Determines Indifferent Things (pp. 30-32)
		- Edwards discusses the will's ability to determine indifferent things
	• Section VI: The Notion of Liberty of Will Consisting in Indifference (pp. 32-36)
		- Edwards explores the concept of liberty of will as indifference
	• Section VII: The Supposed Liberty of the Will as Opposite to All Necessity (pp. 36-41)
		- Edwards argues that the supposed liberty of the will is not truly free
	• Section VIII: The Connection of the Acts of the Will with the Dictates of the Understanding (pp. 41-43)
		- Edwards discusses the relationship between the will and the understanding
	• Section IX: Volition is Necessarily Connected with the Influence of Motives (pp. 43-47)
		- Edwards argues that volition is influenced by motives
	• Section X: The Evidence of God's Certain Foreknowledge of the Volitions of Moral Agents (pp. 47-55)
		- Edwards discusses God's foreknowledge of human volitions
	• Section XI: God's Certain Foreknowledge of the Future Volitions of Moral Agents (pp. 55-66)
		- Edwards argues that God's foreknowledge is inconsistent with contingent volitions
	• Section XII: Whether the Volitions of Moral Agents are Connected with Antecedents or Not (pp. 66-?)
		- Edwards discusses the relationship between human volitions and antecedents

â€¢ ••Part III: Arminian Liberty••
	• Examines the necessity of Arminian liberty in moral agency, virtue, praise, and dispraise
	• Considers whether God's moral excellence is necessary and virtuous

â€¢ ••Section I: God's Moral Excellency••
	• God's moral excellence is necessary, yet virtuous and praiseworthy
	• This is a key point in understanding the relationship between necessity and morality

â€¢ ••Section II: Jesus Christ's Human Soul••
	• The acts of Jesus' human soul were necessarily holy, yet truly virtuous and praise-worthy
	• This section explores the implications of Jesus' human nature for moral agency

â€¢ ••Section III: Moral Necessity and Inability••
	• Moral necessity and inability are consistent with blameworthiness
	• This section uses the example of those given up to sin to illustrate this point

â€¢ ••Section IV: God's Command and Man's Obligation••
	• God's command and man's obligation to obedience are consistent with man's moral inability to obey
	• This section explores the relationship between divine command and human morality

â€¢ ••Section V: Sincere Desires and Endeavours••
	• Sincere desires and endeavours are supposed to excuse non-performance of good actions
	• This section examines the role of intention and effort in moral agency

â€¢ ••Section VI: Liberty of Indifference••
	• Liberty of indifference is not necessary to virtue and is inconsistent with it
	• This section argues that Arminian notions of liberty are incompatible with moral agency

â€¢ ••Section VII: Motivations and Inducements••
	• Arminian notions of moral agency are inconsistent with all motivations and inducements
	• This section explores the implications of Arminianism for understanding human motivation

â€¢ ••Part IV: Basis of Arminian Reasoning••
	• Examines the basis of Arminian reasoning in support of their notion of liberty and moral agency
	• Considers the metaphysical notion of action and agency held by Arminians

â€¢ ••Section V: Objections to the Scheme••
	• Two objections to the scheme of moral necessity and inability are raised
		- Necessity renders all means and endeavours to avoid sin or obtain virtue and holiness vain
		- It makes men no more than machines in affairs of morality and religion

	• Recognized for his contributions to the secular world, beyond theology

â€¢ ••The Book's Purpose••
	• Identifies and refutes subtle logical errors in Arminian theology
	• Reduces errors to a single self-contradiction

â€¢ ••Challenges in Reading Edwards••

	• Personal style includes double and triple negatives

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Introduction to the topic••
	• The question of whether the human will is bound or free is a central issue in theology.
	• Jonathan Edwards' views on this topic are particularly significant.
• ••History of the controversy••
	• There is no record of a major controversy on this topic before the Pelagian controversy of the 5th century.
	• Debates on free will existed before the Pelagian controversy, but none were as prominent.
• ••Pelagius' views••
	• Pelagius believed that humans have the power to do good if they will it.
	• He argued that the weakness of the flesh is a pretext for not doing good.
	• He believed that humans can achieve righteousness on their own.
• ••Pelagius' denial of sin••
	• Pelagius denied the universality of sin, which led to his condemnation at the Council of Carthage.
	• He believed that humans do not need redemption because they can do good on their own.
• ••Augustine's response••
	• Augustine argued that the will is bound by sin and that humans are naturally inclined towards sin.
	• He believed that humans are dead in their trespasses and sins and need grace to be saved.
	• He argued that justification is entirely of God.
• ••Augustine's views on free will••
	• Augustine believed that humans have free will, but it is bound by sin.
	• He argued that humans cannot incline themselves towards God without grace.
• ••Edwards' contribution••
	• Edwards made significant contributions to the topic of free will, including his views on trinitarian communication, creaturely participation, necessitarian dispositionalism, divine priority, and harmonious constitutionalism.
• ••The significance of Edwards' views••
	• Edwards' views on free will shed light on a thorny and controversial topic.
	• His views are particularly significant because they are rigorous and clear.
• ••The implications of Edwards' views••
	• If humans are unable to incline themselves towards righteousness, then how can they be responsible for their unrighteousness?
	• Edwards' views on free will have implications for our understanding of sin, redemption, and justification.
• ••The ongoing relevance of the controversy••
	• The controversy over free will remains a significant issue in theology today.
	• Edwards' views on the topic continue to be relevant and influential.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Problem of Free Will••
	• The concept of free will has been a recurring issue throughout church history
	• Martin Luther and other Reformers argued that human will is bound by sin
• ••Luther's View on Free Will••
	• Luther argued that if human will is completely free, humans take partial credit for salvation
	• He believed that sovereign grace intervenes to make humans righteous
• ••The Reformers' Consensus••
	• Other Reformers, including Calvin and Zwingli, agreed with Luther's views
	• They articulated the concept further, with Calvin writing extensively on the topic
• ••Jonathan Edwards' Background••
	• Edwards was born in 1703 in East Windsor, Connecticut
	• He was the only son of 12 children and graduated from Yale College in 1720
• ••Edwards' Ministry••
	• Edwards became a pastor in Northampton, Massachusetts
	• He was involved in the First Great Awakening, a spiritual revival that swept the area
• ••Edwards' Theological Writing••
	• Edwards is considered one of the greatest intellects in American history
	• His book "Freedom of the Will" is a comprehensive and compelling work
• ••Edwards' View on Free Will••
	• Edwards argued that humans are free to choose what they most desire
	• However, because humans are naturally inclined towards sin, their choices are often destructive
• ••Proof of Original Sin••
	• Edwards cited biblical and natural proof for the existence of original sin
	• He argued that all human history testifies to the fact that humans sin
• ••The Will as the Mind Choosing••
	• Edwards defined the will as the mind choosing
	• This was a unique perspective at the time, as most people did not distinguish between the will and the mind
• ••The Consequences of Free Will••
	• Edwards argued that human freedom is actually a source of bondage and destruction
	• Because humans are free to choose what they most desire, they often choose sin and self-destruction.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Jonathan Edwards' View of the Will••
	• Our choices are not determined by the will itself, but by the mind
	• Our choices are determined by what we think is most desirable at any given moment

â€¢ ••Motives and Decision-Making••
	• We choose one thing over another because our mind chooses what it thinks is best
	• Our choices are based on various considerations or motives that have a certain weight with us

â€¢ ••Rational Decision-Making••
	• As rational persons, we will always choose what seems to us to be the right thing to do
	• If we choose not to do the right thing, we would be insane and not truly choosing

â€¢ ••The Problem of Sin••
	• Because of the fall, we are all dead in our trespasses and sins, desiring only sin by nature
	• What seems to us to be right, proper, and good is often wrong, improper, and bad

â€¢ ••The Need for New Natures••
	• In order to do what God would have us to do, we need to be who God wants us to be
	• We need new natures to be able to choose what pleases God

â€¢ ••Natural Inability••
	• We are naturally unable to do something when we can't do it if we will, because what is most commonly called nature doesn't allow it
	• Examples of natural inability include flying or walking on water

â€¢ ••Moral Inability••
	• Moral inability consists in the opposition or want of inclination
	• We are morally unable to do something when we don't have the desire or inclination to do it

â€¢ ••The Paradox of Human Responsibility••
	• The paradox is that we are responsible to follow God's holy commands, but we are unable to do so
	• This paradox is a problem that has plagued theologians, including Pelagius

â€¢ ••Edwards' Contribution••
	• Edwards distinguished between natural inability and moral inability
	• He argued that we are all naturally able to do what is right, but morally unable to do so because of our sinful nature

â€¢ ••The Role of Grace••
	• We cannot change our own nature, so we need the sovereign hand of grace to change it for us
	• We cannot do what pleases God because we will not do what pleases God, and the reason we will not is because we don't want to.

Here are 10 bullets summarizing the text with sub-bullets:

• ••Understanding the Law of God••
	• We have a physical mouth to praise God
	• We have a will to choose what we want to do
	• Original sin does not eradicate our humanity or ability to make choices
• ••The Natural Ability vs. Moral Ability••
	• We have the natural ability to do what God requires of us
	• We lack the moral ability to choose God
	• We have no desire to obey God or seek Him
• ••The Consequences of the Fall••
	• We have no desire to choose God
	• We are inclined to sin and self-destruction
	• Our nature has to be changed to seek God
• ••The Greatness of the Gospel••
	• The gospel is great because it addresses the ruin caused by the fall
	• The disease of sin requires a great remedy
	• The gospel is good news because it redeems us from our desperate state
• ••Edwards' View of Free Will••
	• Man is free to choose according to his strongest inclinations
	• However, we are naturally inclined to sin and self-destruction
	• Our nature has to be changed to seek God
• ••The Role of God in Redemption••
	• God can justly command our obedience because we have the faculties of choice
	• God can hold us responsible for our choices
	• Only God can liberate us from our captivity to sin
• ••The Importance of Moral Ability••
	• We lack the moral ability to choose God
	• Our decisions follow our inclinations
	• Sin has rendered us hopeless, but this is what makes the gospel great
• ••The Relationship Between Sin and Responsibility••
	• Our responsibility rests on the fact that we possess natural ability
	• We are not excused from responsibility because we lack moral ability
	• Our natural ability is not destroyed by our lack of moral ability
• ••The Greatness of the Gospel (continued)••
	• The gospel is great because it addresses the bad news of our fallen nature
	• The gospel is great because it redeems us from our desperate state
	• The gospel is great because it changes our nature to seek God
• ••Conclusion••
	• Edwards' view of free will emphasizes the importance of moral ability
	• Our nature has to be changed to seek God
	• Only God can liberate us from our captivity to sin.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Critique of labeling others with names••
	• The practice of labeling others with names like Arminian, Arian, or Socinian is considered unjust and groundless.
	• It implies that the person's opinions are based on the named individual, rather than their own study of the Scriptures.

â€¢ ••Comparison to Christians••
	• Christians are called by the name of Christ, whom they regard as their Head and Rule.
	• This is acceptable, whereas labeling others with names is not.

â€¢ ••Arminianism as an example••
	• The followers of Arminius are not necessarily more likely to believe in Arminianism because of Arminius himself.
	• They study the Scriptures and seek truth, regardless of Arminius's opinions.

â€¢ ••Injurious effects of labeling••
	• Labeling others can lead the multitude to imagine that the difference between them and others is greater than it actually is.
	• It can create a sense of distance and disaffection among Christians.

â€¢ ••Uncharitable spirit••
	• The practice of labeling others is often seen as arising from an uncharitable, narrow, and contracted spirit.
	• This spirit inclines people to confine all that is good to themselves and their own party.

â€¢ ••Consequences of labeling••
	• Labeling others can lead to mutual hatred and sustain distance among Christians.
	• It can also lead to an evil temper of mind, being censorious and bitter towards those who differ.

â€¢ ••Counterargument••
	• The author acknowledges that there are unhappy consequences of labeling, but argues that these objections are carried too far.
	• The generality of mankind are disposed to uncharitableness and censoriousness, and this can arise from many things.

â€¢ ••Language and naming••
	• The use of names to distinguish people of different opinions is a natural and necessary part of language.
	• It enables people to express their ideas with ease and expedition.

â€¢ ••Distinguishing opinions••
	• Distinguishing people of different opinions may not imply any more than that there is a difference.
	• It is a defect to suppose that labeling others arises mainly from an uncharitable spirit.

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• The author humbly conceives that the objections to labeling others are carried too far.
	• The practice of labeling others is a complex issue, and its effects should be considered in context.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Definition of the Will••
	• The Will is the faculty of the mind that chooses or prefers something.
	• It is the power or principle of mind that enables it to make decisions.
• ••Act of the Will••
	• An act of the Will is the same as an act of choosing or choice.
	• It involves selecting one thing over another, or preferring something over its absence.
• ••Voluntary Action••
	• Voluntary action is always elective, meaning it involves a choice.
	• The Will determines between two options, making a choice.
• ••Names for the Act of the Will••
	• Choosing, refusing, approving, disapproving, liking, disliking, embracing, rejecting, determining, directing, commanding, forbidding, inclining, being averse, being pleased or displeased with all of these can be reduced to the capacity to choose.
• ••Locke's Definition••
	• Locke defines the Will as a power or ability to prefer or choose.
	• He suggests that preferring is the best way to express the act of volition, but acknowledges that it does not express it precisely.
• ••Immediate Object of the Will••
	• The immediate object of the Will is not an external action, but rather an exertion or alteration of the body.
	• When a person wills to walk, the immediate object of the Will is the exertion or motion of their legs and feet.
• ••Consciousness of the Will••
	• The only thing that a person is conscious of while walking is their preferring or choosing to walk.
	• This preference is made through successive moments, and is accompanied by external sensations and motions.
• ••Instantaneous Consequence••
	• When the soul prefers or chooses an immediate exertion or alteration of the body, it instantaneously follows.
	• There is no delay or intermediate step between the preference and the action.
• ••Experience and the Will••
	• The author has found through experience that on an immediate preference, external sensations and motions do actually, instantaneously, and constantly follow.
	• This suggests that the Will is closely tied to the body and its sensations.
• ••Nature of the Will••
	• The Will is a fundamental aspect of human nature, established by God.
	• It is a power or principle that enables the soul to make decisions and act voluntarily.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Will••
	• According to John Locke, the will is a distinct concept from desire
	• The will is a more general concept that can extend to things both present and absent
	• Desire, on the other hand, is limited to things that are absent

â€¢ ••Relationship between Will and Desire••
	• Locke argues that the will and desire can run counter to each other
	• However, the author disagrees and argues that this is not possible

â€¢ ••Counterexample••
	• Locke provides an example where a person is forced to persuade someone, but desires that the persuasion fails
	• The author argues that this example is flawed and does not demonstrate a genuine conflict between will and desire

â€¢ ••Analysis of the Counterexample••
	• The author argues that in the counterexample, the person's will and desire are not actually in conflict
	• The person's will is to utter words that have a tendency to persuade, and their desire is also to utter those words

â€¢ ••Key Point••
	• The author argues that the will and desire are not distinct concepts that can run counter to each other
	• Instead, they are two aspects of the same thing, and cannot be in conflict

â€¢ ••Locke's Definition of Will••
	• Locke defines the will as "entirely distinguished from desire"
	• However, the author argues that this definition is too narrow and does not capture the full complexity of the will

â€¢ ••Distinction between Will and Preference••
	• The author argues that the will is not the same as preference or liking
	• Instead, the will is a more general concept that can encompass a range of desires and preferences

â€¢ ••Common Speech••
	• In common speech, the will and desire are often used interchangeably
	• However, the author argues that this is not a precise or accurate way to understand the relationship between the two concepts

â€¢ ••Implications of the Relationship between Will and Desire••
	• If the will and desire are not distinct concepts that can run counter to each other, then it is not possible for a person to will something that is contrary to their desires
	• This has implications for our understanding of human behavior and decision-making

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• The author concludes that the will and desire are not distinct concepts that can run counter to each other
	• Instead, they are two aspects of the same thing, and cannot be in conflict.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Locke's Argument••
	• Locke argues that the will and desire are distinct, but Edwards disagrees
	• Edwards suggests that the will chooses between competing desires, as in the case of a police officer who chooses to take a life to preserve their own

â€¢ ••The Will and Desire••
	• The will and desire are not distinct in their nature
	• The will chooses between competing desires, as in the case of a person who desires to sin and also desires not to sin

â€¢ ••Coercion and the Will••
	• When a person is coerced, their will is not free to choose
	• The person is forced to act against their will, which is a logical contradiction unless they had no actual choice

â€¢ ••Desire and Will••
	• Desire and will are not distinct in different things
	• A person may desire to use persuasion and also desire it not to prevail, but this does not prove that desire is distinct from will

â€¢ ••Volition and Choice••
	• In every act of will, there is an act of choice
	• In every volition, there is a preference or prevailing inclination of the soul

â€¢ ••The Determination of the Will••
	• Determining the will means causing the act of will to be one way rather than another
	• The will is said to be determined when its choice is directed to and fixed on a particular object

â€¢ ••The Cause of Determination••
	• The determination of the will supposes an effect, which must have a cause
	• If the will is determined, then there is a determiner, which must be assumed to be intended by those who say the will determines itself

â€¢ ••The Determiner of the Will••
	• The determiner of the will is the subject of inquiry
	• Various opinions have been advanced on this matter, but it is not necessary to examine them all

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Understanding and Will••
	• The question of whether the will always follows the final dictate of the understanding is debated
	• It is sufficient for the author's purpose to say that the determiner of the will is the subject of inquiry

â€¢ ••The Nature of Volition••
	• Volition involves a preference or prevailing inclination of the soul
	• Where there is no absolute preference or choice, but a perfect equilibrium, there is no volition

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Motive••
	• A motive is the whole of whatever moves, excites, or invites the mind to volition (making a choice)
	• A motive can be one thing or many things in conjunction

â€¢ ••Strength of Motive••
	• The strength of a motive refers to its ability to induce a particular act of volition
	• The strength of a motive can be the strength of one thing alone or many things together

â€¢ ••Objectivity of Motive••
	• A motive must be something that is extant in the view or apprehension of the understanding
	• A motive cannot induce the mind to will or act anything if it is not perceived or in the mind's view

â€¢ ••Perception and Motive••
	• Nothing can affect the mind unless it is perceived or in some way in the mind's view
	• What is wholly unperceived and perfectly out of the mind's view cannot affect the mind at all

â€¢ ••Tendency of Motive••
	• A motive has some sort and degree of tendency or advantage to move or excite the Will
	• This tendency is what is meant by the strength of the motive

â€¢ ••Weaker and Stronger Motives••
	• A weaker motive has a lesser degree of previous advantage or tendency to move the Will
	• A stronger motive has a greater degree of previous tendency to excite and induce the choice

â€¢ ••Will and Motive••
	• The will is always determined by the strongest motive
	• The strongest motive is the one that appears most inviting and eligible to the mind

â€¢ ••Nature and Circumstances of Motive••
	• The strength of a motive comes from many things pertaining to the nature and circumstances of the thing viewed
	• The strength of a motive also comes from the nature and circumstances of the mind that views it

â€¢ ••Good and Motive••
	• Whatever is perceived or apprehended by an intelligent and voluntary agent is considered or viewed as good
	• The will is always as the greatest apparent good is

â€¢ ••Sense of Good••
	• When I use the term "good", I mean it in the sense of agreeable or pleasing to the mind
	• To appear good to the mind is the same as to appear agreeable or to seem pleasing to the mind

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of "good"••
	• The word "good" refers to what suits the mind and pleases it most
	• It includes the removal or avoidance of evil or what is disagreeable and uneasy

â€¢ ••The will and apparent good••
	• The will is always directed towards the greatest apparent good
	• This means choosing what appears to be best for us

â€¢ ••Immediate vs. remote objects••
	• The immediate object of the act of volition is what is most directly chosen
	• Remote objects are those to which the act of will has only an indirect and remote respect

â€¢ ••Example: the drunkard••
	• The drunkard's immediate objects are drinking the liquor or letting it alone
	• His choice is based on what is most agreeable to him in the present view of his mind

â€¢ ••Remote objects••
	• The drunkard's remote objects are the present pleasure and future misery associated with drinking
	• These are not the proper objects of the act of volition

â€¢ ••The proper object of the will••
	• The proper object of the will is what is most pleasing to the person in the present moment
	• This is what the person chooses to do

â€¢ ••Choosing between present pleasure and future advantage••
	• If the person chooses a present pleasure over a future advantage, a lesser present pleasure appears more agreeable to them

â€¢ ••The role of uneasiness••
	• Uneasiness determines the will, as it is what the person seeks to avoid or remove
	• This is the same as choosing and seeking what is easier and more agreeable

â€¢ ••Mr. Locke's view••
	• Mr. Locke supposes that uneasiness determines the will
	• This view is consistent with the idea that the will is directed towards the greatest apparent good

â€¢ ••The paradox of the will••
	• The will appears to be directed towards what is good, but often chooses what is evil
	• This is illustrated in Paul's lament in Romans 7:15-20

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••I. The Nature of Volition••

• The will is always determined by what appears most agreeable to the mind.
	• This is not the same as saying the will is determined by the greatest apparent good.
	• The mind's preference and what appears most agreeable are often indistinguishable.

••II. Factors Influencing Volition••

• The apparent nature and circumstances of the object of choice contribute to its agreeableness.
	• This includes:
		1. What appears in the object that renders it beautiful or pleasant.
		2. The apparent degree of pleasure or trouble attending the object.
		3. The apparent state of the pleasure or trouble, including its timeliness.

••III. Timeliness and Volition••

• Timeliness is agreeable to the mind, with speed being more pleasurable than delay.
	• If two objects are equal in degree of pleasure, but one is imminent and the other is postponed, the nearer one will appear more agreeable.

••IV. The Manner of the View••

• The manner of the view also influences the agreeableness of an object of choice.
	• This includes:
		1. The degree of assent with which the mind judges the pleasure to be future.
		2. The probability of the pleasure, with more probable pleasures being more agreeable.

••V. The Influence of the Mind's State••

• The state and circumstances of the mind as it views the object also influence its agreeableness.
	• This includes various factors that are not specified in the text, but may include emotions, motivations, and past experiences.

••VI. The Complexity of Volition••

• Enumerating all the factors that influence volition would be a matter of great difficulty.
	• It may require a separate treatise to fully explore the complexities of volition.

••VII. The Role of Circumstances••

• Circumstances, such as the apparent nature and consequences of the object, are viewed as belonging to the object as it stands in the mind's view.
	• These circumstances should be considered as parts of the object, influencing its agreeableness.

••VIII. The Importance of Perspective••

• The manner of the view, including the degree of assent and probability, influences the agreeableness of the object.
	• This highlights the importance of perspective in shaping our choices and decisions.

••IX. The Relationship Between Volition and Happiness••

• Volition is influenced by what appears most agreeable to the mind, which is often related to happiness.
	• The degree of apparent pleasure or trouble attending the object influences its agreeableness.

••X. The Limits of Language••

• Strict propriety of speech may require distinguishing between the voluntary action and the choice itself.
	• However, the distinction between these two is often blurred, and the language used may not always be precise.

â€¢ ••Defining Necessity, Impossibility, and Inability••
	• These terms are often used in controversies about free will and moral agency.
	• A clear understanding of their meaning is essential for these discussions.

â€¢ ••The Common Use of Necessity and Impossibility••
	• In common speech, necessity and impossibility are relative terms.
	• They relate to supposed opposition or power that is insufficient to hinder or alter a thing.

â€¢ ••The Original Sense of Necessity••
	• Necessity means that something must be, despite any supposed opposition.
	• This is the original and proper sense of the word.

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Necessity and Impossibility••
	• To say that something is necessary is the same as saying it is impossible for it not to be.
	• Impossibility refers to supposed power that is insufficient to bring something to pass.

â€¢ ••The Relative Nature of Necessity and Impossibility••
	• These terms are relative and always refer to supposed opposition or power.
	• They imply something that frustrates an endeavor or desire.

â€¢ ••The Two Senses of Necessity and Impossibility••
	• Necessity and impossibility can be used in two senses:
		- Something is necessary or impossible in general, despite any supposed opposition.
		- Something is necessary or impossible to us, despite all supposed opposition from us.

â€¢ ••The Context of Necessity and Impossibility••
	• These terms are often used in controversies about liberty and moral agency.
	• They relate to the opposition or endeavor of our wills or voluntary exertions.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Understanding of "necessary" and related terms••
	• Something is necessary when we cannot help it, despite our will.
	• This understanding is based on common language usage.

â€¢ ••Common usage of "necessary", "impossible", "irresistible", and "unable"••
	• These terms imply opposition, unwillingness, and resistance.
	• They are used to describe situations where our desires and endeavors are insufficient.

â€¢ ••Association of ideas and words••
	• The idea of something being related to our will, desire, and endeavor is strongly connected to these terms.
	• These ideas and words are associated, making it difficult to separate them.

â€¢ ••Using terms of art in a different sense••
	• Even when using words in a technical sense, we may still slide into their common usage.
	• This can lead to inconsistent and confusing reasoning and discourse.

â€¢ ••Proper meaning of terms••
	• When these terms are used in cases where the will is not assumed to be insufficient, they are not used in their proper meaning.
	• In such cases, the terms are either used nonsensically or in a new sense that differs from their original meaning.

â€¢ ••Examples of improper use of terms••
	• Affirming that it is necessary for a man to choose virtue rather than vice, when he prefers virtue to vice.
	• Using terms to describe situations where there is no opposition, unwillingness, or resistance.

â€¢ ••Philosophers' and metaphysicians' use of terms••
	• They often use these terms in a sense different from their common and original meaning.
	• They apply them to cases where no opposition is supposed, such as God's existence before creation.

â€¢ ••Application of terms to divine and created beings••
	• Philosophers and metaphysicians use these terms to describe the inclinations and actions of God and created intelligent beings.
	• They apply them to cases where all opposition of the will is excluded in the very supposition of the case.

â€¢ ••Implications of using terms in a different sense••
	• Using terms in a new sense can lead to confusion and inconsistency in reasoning and discourse.
	• It is essential to be circumspect when using technical terms to avoid sliding into their common usage.

â€¢ ••Importance of understanding the proper meaning of terms••
	• Understanding the proper meaning of terms is crucial for clear and consistent reasoning and discourse.
	• It is essential to recognize when terms are being used in a new sense that differs from their original meaning.

â€¢ ••Definition of Philosophical Necessity••
	• Not the same as certainty of knowledge
	• Refers to the inherent connection between a subject and predicate in a proposition

â€¢ ••Limitations of Traditional Definitions••
	• Definitions that rely on "can" or "cannot" are circular and unclear
	• These terms are relative and depend on the context of power or ability

â€¢ ••Philosophical Necessity as a Connection••
	• Philosophical necessity is the full and fixed connection between a subject and predicate
	• This connection is the foundation of the certainty of a proposition

â€¢ ••Types of Connections••
	• (1) The subject and predicate may have a perfect and necessary connection in themselves
		- Examples: mathematical truths, metaphysical truths, and logical contradictions
	• (2) The connection may be fixed and certain due to external factors
		- Examples: the existence of something that has a necessary connection to its circumstances

â€¢ ••Examples of Necessary Connections••
	• The eternal existence of being is necessary in itself
	• God's infinity and other attributes are necessary by their own nature
	• Mathematical truths, such as 2•2=4, are necessary in and of themselves

â€¢ ••Necessity in Human Behavior••
	• It is necessary that men should do to others as they would have others do to them

â€¢ ••Necessity in Metaphysical and Mathematical Truths••
	• Innumerable metaphysical and mathematical truths are necessary in and of themselves

â€¢ ••The Role of Necessity in Propositions••
	• When the subject and predicate of a proposition have a full and certain connection, the existence of the thing affirmed is necessary

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Necessity and Liberty••
	• The text aims to prove that necessity is not inconsistent with liberty

â€¢ ••The Significance of Philosophical Necessity••
	• Philosophical necessity is a fundamental concept that underlies the certainty of knowledge and the nature of reality

	• The only thing lacking is a disposition, or a will

	• The only thing lacking is a disposition, or a will

	• These habits can be overcome by making an alteration in future actions

	• It implies that they cannot will if they do will, which is a false statement

••1. Definition of Liberty••
	• Liberty is the power or opportunity to do as one pleases
	• It means being free from hindrance or impediment in doing something as one wills

	• The contrary of Liberty is being hindered or unable to conduct something as one wills
	• It is also being required to do otherwise

	• To have Liberty or its contrary, one must have a faculty or power called will

	• The Will itself is not an Agent that has a will
	• The power of choosing is not an Agent that has a power to choose
	• The person or soul is the Agent who possesses the Will

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Problem with Self-Determination••
	• The faculty or power of will determines its own volitions without any prior act.
	• This is an evasion that is full of absurdity.
• ••The First Objection••
	• If the power of will determines an act of volition, it is the same as the soul determining volition by an act of will.
	• Therefore, to say that the power of will determines volition without an act of will preceding it is a contradiction.
• ••The Second Objection••
	• If a power of will determines the act of the will, then a power of choosing determines it.
	• But if a power of choosing determines volition, it does so by choosing it, which means there is an antecedent choice.
• ••The Third Objection••
	• To say that the faculty or soul determines its own volition but not by any act is a contradiction.
	• Because the soul must act to direct, decide, or determine anything.
• ••The Sovereignty of the Will••
	• Advocates for the freedom of the will speak of a certain sovereignty in the will.
	• This sovereignty implies that the will has power to determine its own volition.
• ••The Determination of Volition••
	• The determination of volition must itself be an act of the will.
	• Otherwise, it cannot be an exercise of the supposed power and sovereignty of the will.
• ••The Dilemma••
	• If the will is active in determining its volitions, then the determination is an act of the will.
	• But if the will is not active, then how does it exercise any liberty in determining its acts?
• ••The Absurdity of Self-Determination••
	• If there is an act of the will in determining all its own free acts, then one free act is determined by another.
	• This leads to the absurdity of every free act being determined by a foregoing free act.
• ••The Absence of Liberty••
	• If there is no act or exercise of the will in determining its own acts, then no liberty is exercised in determining them.
	• This means that no liberty consists in the power of the will to determine its own acts.
• ••The Overthrow of the Scheme••
	• The scheme of liberty consisting in self-determining power is overthrown by either part of the dilemma.
	• Therefore, the notion of the freedom of the will is flawed.

	• The text argues that liberty cannot consist in a self-determining power of the will.

• ••Three Possible Interpretations••
	• The text presents three possible interpretations of how the will determines its own volitions:
		- (1) The determining act is before the determined act in the order of nature, but not in the order of time.
		- (2) The determining act is not distinct from the determined act, but is the same as its exertion.
		- (3) Volition has no cause and is no effect, but comes into existence with a particular determination.

	• If the determining act is before the determined act in the order of nature, but not in the order of time, it still proves that the determining act is distinct from and independent of the determined act.
	• This is because the cause of a particular motion or effect is distinct from the motion or effect itself, just as an architect is distinct from the house they build.

	• If the determining act is distinct from the determined act, and occurs before it in the order of nature, then we can go back to the first act in the series, which has no determining act preceding it.

	• Someone might argue that the determining act is not distinct from the determined act, but is the same as its exertion.
	• This means that when the soul exerts a particular volition, it causes and determines that act of volition.

••1. Definition of Indifference••
• Indifference refers to the soul's power or ability to choose, not the choice itself.
• It's about the imminence of an action, not the action itself.

••2. Refining the Concept of Indifference••
• Some writers refine the concept of indifference to mean the soul's power or ability to choose is indifferent.
• However, this distinction is newly invented and doesn't align with the manner of expression used by defenders of Liberty of Indifference.

••3. Meaning of Indifference••
• The indifference of the soul as to its ability or power to will is the same as the indifference of the state of the power or faculty of the will.
• It refers to the soul's state of being free from actual possession and predetermination.

••4. Arminian View of Indifference••
• Arminians view indifference as essential to Liberty of Will.
• It leaves the will not already determined, but free from actual possession and predetermination.

••5. Perfect Indifference Required••
• For the scheme of Liberty to work, indifference must be perfect and absolute.
• There must be a complete freedom from all antecedent preponderance or inclination.

••6. Antecedent Preponderance••
• If the will is already inclined before exerting its sovereign power, its inclination is not wholly attributable to itself.
• A prior inclination must be removed for the will to be in a state of Liberty for mere self-determination.

••7. Inconsistency with Notion of Liberty••
• The least degree of an antecedent bias is inconsistent with the notion of liberty.
• A prior inclination binds the will, making it impossible to act other than agreeably to that inclination.

••8. Will's Inclination••
• The will preponderates and inclines to what it prefers.
• It's equally impossible for the will to choose contrary to its own remaining and present preponderating inclination.

••9. Freedom of the Will••
• The will is not at Liberty for a new free act or for self-determination as long as it's under the influence of an old preponderating inclination.
• The will's freedom consists in (or depends upon) this vacancy and opportunity left for the will to determine the act.

••10. Implications of Imperfect Indifference••
• If the will is not in a state of perfect indifference, it's not in a state of Liberty for mere self-determination.
• The will's freedom is compromised if it's already inclined or has a prior bias.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Mr. Chubb's argument••
	• Mr. Chubb believes that motive or excitement is the ground of the being of volition.
	• He thinks that volition cannot take place without some previous ground or motive to induce it.
• ••Passive ground of action••
	• Mr. Chubb speaks of motive as a passive ground of action.
	• He says that volition comes from the influence of the motive, and that the motive is passive with regard to the volition.
• ••Absurdity of the argument••
	• Mr. Chubb's argument is absurd because it creates a circular reasoning.
	• If every volition must have a previous motive, then the very first volition must be excited by a previous motive, which is passive.
• ••Distinction between two volitions••
	• Mr. Chubb tries to distinguish between two volitions: one that yields to the invitation of a motive, and another that chooses something.
	• However, this distinction does not help the matter because the two volitions are essentially the same.
• ••Prior volition••
	• If we suppose a prior volition that is distinct from the one that yields to the motive, then that prior volition must also have a previous motive.
	• This creates an infinite regress, where every volition must have a previous motive, and so on.
• ••Infinite regress••
	• The argument creates an infinite regress, where every volition must have a previous motive, and that motive must have a previous motive, and so on.
	• This is absurd because it implies that there is no first volition that is not caused by a previous motive.
• ••Mr. Chubb's inconsistency••
	• Mr. Chubb insists on clearness of meaning in others, but his own argument is unclear and inconsistent.
	• He should have explained his meaning in the phrase "passive ground of action" to show that it is not confused and inconsistent.
• ••Alternative supposition••
	• If we suppose that Mr. Chubb does not mean passive with regard to the volition that it is the ground of, but some other antecedent volition, then it would not help the matter.
	• This is because the argument would still create an infinite regress, where every volition must have a previous motive.
• ••Volition and motive••
	• Mr. Chubb asserts that every volition has some motive, and that in the nature of the thing, no volition can take place without some motive to induce it.
	• However, this assertion is inconsistent with his own argument, which implies that the motive is passive with regard to the volition.
• ••Conclusion••
	• Mr. Chubb's argument is absurd because it creates a circular reasoning and an infinite regress.
	• He should have explained his meaning in the phrase "passive ground of action" to show that it is not confused and inconsistent.

• ••Mr. Chubb's argument is challenged••

• ••The concept of previous tendency••
	• The author questions whether an event can follow an antecedent without any previous tendency to that event.
	• If an event follows an antecedent without a previous tendency, it suggests that the event follows without any reason or motive.
• ••The role of the mind in preference••
	• The mind acts with reference to two or more things that are compared and stand in competition with each other.
	• In a comparative act, the mind prefers what appears to be inferior, acting absolutely without motive or inducement.
• ••The example of a hungry man••
	• A hungry man is offered two sorts of food, both of which he has an appetite for.
	• He chooses the one for which he has the least appetite, refusing the one for which he has the strongest appetite.
• ••The independence of volition from motive••
	• If the mind can go beyond motive, it can also go without motive.
	• This demonstrates the independence of volition from motive.

	• A comparative act is one in which the mind acts with reference to two or more things that are compared.
	• In a comparative act, the mind prefers what appears to be inferior, acting absolutely without motive or inducement.

	• The author uses the example of a hungry man to illustrate the concept of comparative acts.
	• The man's appetite is the only factor that induces him to choose one option over the other.

	• If the mind can go without motive, it suggests that volition is independent of motive.
	• This challenges the idea that volition always requires a motive to induce it.
• ••The conclusion••
	• The author argues that the concept of previous tendency is inconsistent with the idea of an act of preference.
	• The independence of volition from motive is demonstrated, challenging Mr. Chubb's assertion.

â€¢ ••Mr. Chubb's Hypothesis••
	• He supposes that the Will cannot stir without a Motive.
	• He also supposes that if there is a Motive to one thing and none to the contrary, then volition will infallibly follow that Motive.

â€¢ ••Contradictions in Mr. Chubb's Hypothesis••
	• He supposes that the Will is entirely dependent on Motives, but also that it can use Motives as it pleases.
	• He supposes that the Will is wholly independent of Motives, but also that it can choose its own influence from them.

â€¢ ••Implications of Mr. Chubb's Hypothesis••
	• Volition must be without any previous ground in any Motive.
	• The difference between various acts of the Will is plainly without any previous ground in either of the Motives.

â€¢ ••The Source of Different Consequences••
	• The source of this different consequence must be sought elsewhere, as perfect identity in the ground of each Motive cannot be the reason why the same consequence does not follow from each.

â€¢ ••Mr. Chubb's Insistence on Motives••
	• He insists that no volition can take place without some Motive to induce it.

â€¢ ••Necessity and Agency••

	• He thinks that to suppose someone can be an agent in doing what is necessary is a plain contradiction.

â€¢ ••Mr. Chubb's View of Liberty and Necessity••
	• He supposes that necessity cannot be consistent with agency or freedom.
	• He thinks that to suppose otherwise is to make Liberty and Necessity, Activity and passivity, the same thing.

â€¢ ••The Balance Analogy••
	• Mr. Chubb's hypothesis is compared to a balance that moves itself when there is no weight in either scale.
	• The agency or activity of the balance is sufficient to move itself against a greater weight, and must certainly be more than sufficient to move it when there is no weight at all.

â€¢ ••The Will's Independence••
	• Mr. Chubb supposes that the Will is not determined by any previous superior strength of the Motive.

â€¢ ••The Problem of Motive and Volition••
	• Mr. Chubb's hypothesis creates a problem of how to explain the difference between various acts of the Will.
	• The source of this difference must be sought elsewhere, as perfect identity in the ground of each Motive cannot be the reason why the same consequence does not follow from each.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Problem of Infinite Regression••
	• Mr. Chubb's idea that every free act of choice is the product of a free act of choice leads to an infinite regression
	• This means that there would be an infinite number of free acts in succession, with no beginning
	• This is absurd, as it implies that no single act of choice is truly free

â€¢ ••The Absurdity of Necessary Acts••
	• According to Mr. Chubb's principles, every act of choice would be necessary, as it would be the effect of a prior act
	• This means that every act would be connected to a prior cause, making it impossible for any act to be truly free

â€¢ ••The Contradiction of Free and Necessary Acts••
	• Mr. Chubb's notion of a free act is a contradiction, as it is both free and necessary
	• This is because every free act is the product of a prior free act, making it necessary

â€¢ ••The Infinite Chain of Acts••
	• According to Mr. Chubb's principles, there would be an infinite number of free acts in succession
	• Each act would be necessary, as it would be the effect of a prior act
	• This means that none of the acts in the infinite chain would be truly free

â€¢ ••The Impossibility of Reward or Punishment••
	• Since every act is necessary, it is impossible to reward or punish the agent for any single act
	• The agent is active in all acts, but passive in none, making it impossible to hold them accountable

â€¢ ••Mr. Chubb's Denial of Motives as Causes••
	• Mr. Chubb denies that motives are the causes of the acts of the will
	• He argues that if the will is moved by motives, it would not be a self-moving principle

â€¢ ••The Absurdity of a Self-Moving Principle••
	• Mr. Chubb argues that it is absurd to say that a self-moving principle is moved by a cause external to itself
	• This is because a self-moving principle is, by definition, not moved by external causes

â€¢ ••The Role of Motives in Exciting the Will••

	• This implies that motives are the cause of the will's being excited

â€¢ ••The Necessity of Motives for Volition••
	• Mr. Chubb says that volition cannot take place without motives to excite it
	• This implies that motives are necessary for the will to be active

â€¢ ••Definition of Exciting••
	• To excite is to cause something to be excited
	• It is a positive influence that brings something into existence

â€¢ ••Mr. Chubb's Definition of Motives••
	• Motives are the ground and reason of action by influence
	• They are the cause of an action through prevailing influence

â€¢ ••Inconsistency in Mr. Chubb's View••
	• Mr. Chubb says motives have a prevailing influence to produce an action
	• Yet he claims it is absurd to say motives are causes of an action

â€¢ ••Mr. Chubb's Definition of Disposing Influence••
	• Motives dispose the agent to action by their influence
	• This means that one motive prevails over others to produce an action

â€¢ ••Consequence of Disposing Influence••
	• If motives dispose the mind to action, they cause the mind to be willing
	• This means that motives cause the act of the Will

â€¢ ••Inconsistency in Mr. Chubb's View (continued)••
	• Mr. Chubb says motives are the necessary ground and reason of the acts of the Will

â€¢ ••Consequence of Mr. Chubb's View••
	• Mr. Chubb's view is inconsistent with the idea that motives cause the act of the Will
	• This inconsistency is due to his notion of Liberty, which is void of necessity

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Mr. Chubb's view is impossible to make consistent with the influence of motives in volition
	• A due consideration of these things may convince us that his notion of Liberty is flawed.

	• This impossibility is the immediate ground of the certainty of the knowledge

	• This certainty arises from the present impossibility of the non-existence of the affirmed event

Here are 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Certainty of Knowledge••
	• Certainty of knowledge is knowing or discerning the certainty that exists in known things themselves.
	• This certainty is based on the firm and infallible connection between the subject and predicate of a proposition.
• ••God's Foreknowledge••
	• God's certain foreknowledge of the future existence of an event is his view of the firm and indissoluble connection between the subject and predicate of the proposition that affirms its future existence.
	• The subject is the possible event, and the predicate is its actual future existence.
• ••Necessity of the Event••
	• If future existence itself is firmly and indissolubly connected with the event, then the future existence of that event is necessary.
	• If God certainly knows the future existence of an event that is wholly contingent, then he sees a firm connection between a subject and predicate that are not firmly connected, which is a contradiction.
• ••Causation and Foreknowledge••
	• Causation is a separate and unrelated issue from foreknowledge.
	• Foreknowledge supposes and proves that the thing known must come to pass, or else God's foreknowledge is neither certain nor infallible.
• ••Certainty of Futurity••
	• Knowledge of futurity supposes futurity.
	• A certain knowledge of futurity supposes a certain futurity preceding that certain knowledge.
• ••Prior Impossibility••
	• There is no certain futurity of something antecedent to any certainty of knowledge other than a prior impossibility that the thing should prove untrue.
	• This prior impossibility is the necessity of the event.
• ••Effect of Foreknowledge••
	• If God's foreknowledge is not the cause but the effect of the existence of the event that is foreknown, this does not show that foreknowledge does not infer the necessity of the existence of the event.
	• Rather, it shows that the existence of the event is settled and firm, as if it had already been in effect.
• ••Cause and Effect••
	• The effect supposes the cause, and it depends entirely upon it.
	• The effect cannot be more firm and stable than its cause, ground, and reason for being.
• ••Illustration••
	• The appearances and images of things in a mirror or reflecting telescope are the real effects of heavenly bodies that these images resemble.
	• If this is the case, then the images in the telescope have had a past actual existence, making it utterly impossible for them not to have existed.
• ••Conclusion••
	• Foreknowledge supposes and proves that the thing known must come to pass, or else God's foreknowledge is neither certain nor infallible.
	• The necessity of the event is the prior impossibility that the thing should prove untrue.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. The Nature of Heavenly Bodies and Their Effects••

• The existence of heavenly bodies is connected to and dependent on their effects.
• The effects of heavenly bodies are real and necessary, proving the existence of the bodies is equally sure and firm.

••2. The Concept of Future Existences and Their Influence••

• Future existences can influence past events and produce effects beforehand.
• These effects are real and dependent on the future existences, proving their existence is equally sure and firm.

••3. The Case of Images in a Mirror••

• The existence of images in a mirror is dependent on the future existences that produce them.
• This illustrates that the existence of the cause is equally sure and firm as the effect.

••4. The Case of Ideas in the Divine Mind••

• Ideas of future existences in the Divine Mind are properly effects themselves.
• They are truly and properly connected with their cause, proving the existence of the cause is equally sure and firm.

••5. The Argument Against Arminianism••

• Some Arminians argue that God's prescience does not imply a strict succession of events.
• However, this argument is flawed, as God's knowledge is not reactive or passive, but active and causative.

••6. The Nature of God's Knowledge••

• God's knowledge is not subject to succession or change.
• God's knowledge is always infallible and certain, and there is no event that God is ever uncertain of.

••7. The Implication of God's Knowledge••

• Because God always sees things as they are in truth, there is never anything contingent in reality.
• Future events are always in God's view as evident, clear, sure, and necessary as if they already were.

••8. The Concept of Foreknowledge••

• Foreknowledge is often misunderstood as implying a strict succession of events.
• However, God's knowledge is not subject to succession, and future events are always in God's view as certain and necessary.

••9. The Relationship Between God's Knowledge and Events••

• God's knowledge is not a passive reception of information, but an active and causative force.
• God's knowledge is connected to and dependent on the events that occur, and vice versa.

••10. The Implication of God's Certain Foreknowledge••

• God's certain foreknowledge implies that the events that occur are necessary and firm.
• This challenges the idea that human volitions are free and not predetermined by God's knowledge.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Knowledge is Perfect and Unchangeable••
	• God views things perfectly and unchangeably
	• There is no succession in God's ideas or judgment
	• This does not hinder the necessity of moral actions

â€¢ ••God's Knowledge of Future Events••
	• God foreknows the future voluntary actions of men
	• God's knowledge is infallible, and the expression of that knowledge is also infallible
	• There is an infallible connection between the written prediction and the event

â€¢ ••Necessity of Future Events••
	• It is impossible for the prediction and the event to ever disagree
	• This is the same as saying that it is impossible for the event not to come to pass
	• This is the same as saying that its coming to pass is necessary

â€¢ ••No Succession in God's Mind••
	• The objection that God's knowledge has no succession does not weaken the proof
	• The reason for this is that God's knowledge is absolutely perfect and without succession
	• This is because God's knowledge is not altered by the actual existence of the thing known

â€¢ ••Strength of the Demonstration••
	• The objection actually sets the argument in the strongest light
	• It supposes that the existence of future events is as sure as if they had already occurred
	• This means that when they do come to actually exist, it does not make the least alteration or variation in God's knowledge of them

â€¢ ••Immutability of God's Knowledge••

	• This directly demonstrates the thing insisted on: that it is utterly impossible for the known events to fail to exist

â€¢ ••Change in God's Knowledge••
	• If a change in God's knowledge and view of things were possible, then a change in God's mind would be possible
	• This would mean that God would see his former mistake and change his mind
	• This is impossible, as God's knowledge is infallible and unchangeable

â€¢ ••No Alteration in God's Knowledge••
	• The actual existence of the thing known does not increase or add to the clearness or certainty of the thing that is known
	• God calls the things that are not as though they were (Rom 4.17)
	• This means that all things, whether past, present, or future, are viewed with equal evidence and fullness

â€¢ ••Strength of the Argument••
	• The objection actually strengthens the argument
	• It shows that the existence of future events is as sure as if they had already occurred
	• This means that the argument is even more convincing than before

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• The argument shows that it is impossible for future events to fail to exist
	• This is because God's knowledge is infallible and unchangeable
	• The objection actually strengthens the argument and demonstrates the thing insisted on.

	• Arminian objections to this doctrine are unfounded and imply a lack of understanding of the Calvinist position
	• Human liberty is compatible with necessity, as long as the necessity is not coercive.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Problem of Free Will••
	• The author argues that if the will is the spring of all design, then it cannot always be the effect of design.
	• This leads to the conclusion that the will's acts must sometimes come to pass by chance.

â€¢ ••The Infinite Regression Problem••
	• If the will designs to choose whatever it chooses, then it must design to determine all its designs.
	• This creates an infinite regression of designs, with each design being the effect of a foregoing design.

â€¢ ••The First Design Problem••
	• The first design must be the effect of a foregoing design, or else it must be by chance.
	• This creates a problem for the concept of free will, as it is unclear how the first design came to be.

â€¢ ••Alternative Explanations••
	• The author proposes an alternative explanation for human liberty, which involves the connection of the will's acts to some foregoing cause.
	• This alternative explanation is consistent with necessity, but it undermines the Arminian notion of liberty.

â€¢ ••The Consistency Problem••
	• Consistency with necessity directly subverts the Arminian notion of liberty.
	• If human liberty is inconsistent with necessity, then it must consist in freedom from restraint, limitation, and government.

â€¢ ••The Notion of Liberty••
	• The notion of liberty that is commonly conceived is a dignity or privilege worth claiming.
	• However, this notion of liberty is inconsistent with the full and perfect sovereignty of the will over its own determinations.

â€¢ ••The Sovereignty of the Will••
	• The will's sovereignty over its own determinations is incompatible with the idea of liberty as freedom from restraint.
	• The will's sovereignty is also incompatible with the idea of liberty as freedom to act at random.

â€¢ ••The Problem of Moral Agency••
	• If human actions are necessary, then virtue and vice must be empty names.
	• We cannot blame a person for doing only what they could not help, or praise them for what they could not avoid.

â€¢ ••The Virtue and Agency of God••
	• The author argues that God's moral excellency is necessary, yet it is virtuous and praiseworthy.
	• This creates a problem for the concept of moral agency, as it is unclear how God's actions can be virtuous and praiseworthy if they are necessary.

â€¢ ••The Connection to Moral Agency••
	• The author argues that the Arminian notion of liberty is not necessary to moral agency, virtue, and vice.
	• The author suggests that moral agency can exist even if human actions are necessary.

••1. Arminian Logic Criticized••

• It implies that God cannot choose between good and evil, which is a fundamental aspect of human free will.

••2. God's Goodness••
• Arminians suppose that God is necessarily good and gracious.
• They argue that Calvinist doctrines are false because they are not consistent with God's goodness.

••3. Impossibility of God's Evil••
• If it were possible for God to be evil, then the impossibility of Calvinist doctrines ceases.
• This implies that God's goodness is not a necessary attribute.

••4. Virtue in God••
• Virtue in God is not rewardable in the classical sense.
• It is not because God lacks merit, but because he is infinitely happy and above all capacity to receive rewards.

••5. Worthy of Benevolence••
• God is worthy of our supreme benevolence for his virtue.
• He would be worthy of our beneficence if only our goodness could extend to him.

••6. Thankfulness to God••
• We should thank God for his goodness, but not because he is forced to be good.
• We should thank him because he acts freely and of good will.

••7. Beneficence to Others••
• We should requite God's kindness by showing beneficence to others.
• This is especially true for our indigent brethren.

••8. Jesus' Moral Conduct••
• The moral conduct and practices of Jesus Christ are worthy of examination.
• His holy behavior was necessary, virtuous, and worthy of praise.

••9. Jesus' Subject to Law••
• Jesus was subject to law, precept, and commands.
• He was also subject to promises and rewards.

••10. Jesus' State of Trial••
• Jesus was in a state of trial, which is a necessary condition for moral conduct.
• His holy behavior was properly virtuous and worthy of praise.

â€¢ ••Liberty Does Not Consist in Suspending Action••
	• The supposition that liberty consists in a power to suspend action for the present is irrelevant
	• This supposition does not address the issue of whether the will is free or not

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Virtue and vice cannot be found in good or bad dispositions, acting from dispositions, or acting without previous inclination
	• Arminian notions of moral agency and liberty are inconsistent with all motivations and inducements.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. God's Sovereignty••

• God has supreme authority and power over all things.
• All other power is derived from Him and is absolutely dependent on Him.

••2. God's Will••

• God's will is supreme and underived, determined by His own counsel.
• His will is not subject to or restrained by the will of any other being.

••3. God's Wisdom••

• God's wisdom is supreme, perfect, and underived.
• His wisdom determines His will in all things.

••4. God's Sovereignty is Absolute••

• God's sovereignty is absolute and not subject to any superior or equal authority.
• His will is determined by His own infinite and all-sufficient wisdom.

••5. God's Will is Necessary••

• God's will is necessarily determined to what is most wise and best.
• This is not a disadvantage or indignity, but rather a glory and greatness of the Divine Sovereign.

••6. Contingence is Imperfection••

• To suppose that God's will is liable to be carried out at random by blind contingence is a sign of imperfection and meanness.
• This would be unworthy of the Deity.

••7. Freedom from Moral Necessity••

• To be perfectly free from the direction of understanding and to be left to senseless contingence would be the supreme glory.
• This would be a great dignity and advantage.

••8. God's Will is Not Dependent••

• God's supremely wise volition is necessary, but this does not argue for any dependence of His will.
• His existence, infinite perfection, and infinite happiness are also necessary and not dependent.

••9. God's Honor••

• It is not to God's dishonor to be necessarily wise, holy, or to act holily and wisely.
• These are all signs of His glory and greatness.

••10. Conclusion••

• God's sovereignty is absolute and not subject to any superior or equal authority.
• His will is determined by His own infinite and all-sufficient wisdom, and this is a glory and greatness of the Divine Sovereign.
